## Star Wars Insider #45 (1999)

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): Page contents: George Lucas press conference: Qui-Gon not disappearing and Obi-Wan's "more powerful" line as the key (logged); midi-chlorians as metaphor of symbiosis; Anakin "conception by metaphor". (hand-read)

Our notes: `notes/materials/038-star-wars-insider/045-ins045.md:95`

> Han: ‘I ain’t in this for your revolution ... I expect to be well paid.’  Princess Leia: ‘If money is all that you love, then that’s all you’ll receive.’  Luke: ‘I want to come with you to Alderaan, I want to learn the ways of the force and become a Jedi like my father.’  Obi-Wan: ‘You can’t win, Darth - if you strike me down I shall become more powerful than you could possibly imagine.’  MUSICAL NOTES: The rough edit of Star Wars had a temporary track which used pieces of Gustav Holst’s The Planets suite, snatches of Alex North’s score for Cleopatra (Joseph L Mankiewicz, 1963) and selections from Bernard Hermann’s music for Alfred Hitchcock. Whilst these stock tracks helped create the right mood, there was never - as has been suggested since - the possibility of actually releasing the film with such a track. Lucas wanted a rich, orchestral score, something old-fashioned and outdated at the time. He knew it should be reminiscent of Erich Wolfgang Korngold, the multi-award-winning film composer who had scored The Adventures of Robin Hood (Michael Curtiz, 1938) and The Sea Hawk (Michael Curtiz, 1940), two of his models for Star Wars.
>
> Strike me down and I'll become more, more... *(Stops to recall the line.)*
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> Obi-Wan: ‘You can’t win, Darth - if you strike me down I shall become more powerful than you could possibly imagine.’ *(Laughs.)*
— Source: Star Wars Insider #45 (1999).

Note (our notes, around the quote): Qui-Gon Jinn; Obi-Wan Kenobi — entry/section: Lucas: the ability to disappear and Ben's "more powerful than you could possibly imagine" line will be explained: "The Qui-Gon Jinn thing you will discover, as time goes on. There is a whole issue around that and the ability to disappear. The key line which you will use to understand this is when Ben Kenobi tells Darth Vader, "Strike me down and I'll become more, more..." (Stops to recall the line.) "...More powerful than you could possibly imagine." (Laughs.) Hey, he's the fan magazine — what do I know about these things? (Laughs.) But that's a key line, and it will be explained as we go along." Source: *Star Wars Insider #45* (1999), scan p.18

> There is no try.’  Yoda: ‘Excitement? Adventure? A Jedi craves not these things.’  Yoda: ‘If you choose the quick and easy path, as Vader did, you will become a servant of evil.’  Vader: ‘The force is with you, young Skywalker, but you are not a Jedi yet.’  MUSICAL NOTES: When cutting Empire Lucas initially noted where he wanted the Williams score to go; later it became obvious that it would be easier to note where there shouldn’t be music rather than where there should. New themes Williams introduced to the Star Wars universe in this film include the hectic ‘Asteroid field’ and - most famously - ‘The Imperial March’, often referred to as ‘Darth Vader’s Theme’. A bombastic, martial refrain full of suspended notes and malevolence, it is - after the opening theme the series’ most familiar piece of music. Also new is ‘Han Solo and the Princess’ - a lush romantic melody most noticeably used during the quieter scenes on the Millennium Falcon during the flight from Hoth - and a warm, woodwind-heavy theme for Yoda which segues easily into ‘The Force Theme’ from Star Wars.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #49 (2000).

> With Obi, he's known him for a very long time. He knew Qui-Gon; they were contemporaries. He understood Qui-Gon's faults and admired his qualities, and he sees that Obi-Wan has taken on some of his strong-headedness—number one, by wanting to train Anakin. But Anakin is starting to display some interesting characteristics that need to be watched more closely. Obi-Wan intuitively has sensed that and understands our trepidation at wanting Anakin trained. He's sensing that now. But once he's in the program, per se, I think that we watch him more closely and hope that he'll outgrow those things or intuit that he shouldn't do certain things a certain way.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #55 (2001).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Mace Windu; Obi-Wan Kenobi; Qui-Gon Jinn; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Samuel L. Jackson on Mace's relationship with Obi-Wan and Anakin (verified on crop): ""With Obi, he's known him for a very long time. He knew Qui-Gon; they were contemporaries. He understood Qui-Gon's faults and admired his qualities, and he sees that Obi-Wan has taken on some of his strong-headedness—number one, by wanting to train Anakin. But Anakin is starting to display some interesting characteristics that need to be watched more closely. Obi-Wan intuitively has sensed that and understands our trepidation at wanting Anakin trained. He's sensing that now. But once he's in the program, per se, I think that we watch him more closely and hope that he'll outgrow those things or intuit that he shouldn't do certain things a certain way." —Samuel L. Jackson, Star Wars Insider 55, p. 31" Source: *Star Wars Insider #55* (2001), scan p.23

> I am the key to the future. I know you thought I was going to say some hero figure like Captain Kirk, but the future cannot be saved by James T. Kirk. I, Darth Vader, am the hope of the future. Why? My children, Luke and Leia, had the courage to do what I did not do. They resisted temptation; they did not fall to the easy, evil way of the dark side. They had the courage to fight for the Light! They stood and fought for justice, freedom, and right. They even helped me change my ways. There is the hope for the future. Yes, you see the answer lies within my children. I am here today to tell you, in that respect, you are all my children.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #55 (2001).

> I had a meeting with George where he described how Threepio came about. And he said, 'You have a very small part, it's just to say hello.' I was thrilled to be asked anyway. ... Then he told me what Threepio would look like and where he would be from. I thought he was telling me that Alec Guinness had made my character, and I was really, really pleased. I thought, 'Alec was such a good friend that I think it's right that I was made by him.' A couple of days later I suddenly realized that Anakin Skywalker was not played by Alec Guinness. I'm not very good on all these names. I thought, 'Well, that really is interesting, that he's made by Anakin Skywalker, and he's a little boy.'
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Star Wars Insider #56 (2001).
Note (our notes, around the quote): C-3PO; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Anthony Daniels on learning C-3PO's origin for Episode I: ""I had a meeting with George where he described how Threepio came about. And he said, 'You have a very small part, it's just to say hello.' I was thrilled to be asked anyway. ... Then he told me what Threepio would look like and where he would be from. I thought he was telling me that Alec Guinness had made my character, and I was really, really pleased. I thought, 'Alec was such a good friend that I think it's right that I was made by him.' A couple of days later I suddenly realized that Anakin Skywalker was not played by Alec Guinness. I'm not very good on all these names. I thought, 'Well, that really is interesting, that he's made by Anakin Skywalker, and he's a little boy.'" —Anthony Daniels, Star Wars Insider 56, pp. 49-50" Source: *Star Wars Insider #56* (2001), scan p.43

> When I was a kid, it was always Han Solo. Then later on in life, it became Darth Vader, who's far more tragic a figure. Han Solo had all the bluster, so when you're a kid, you kind of dig on that. But as you get older, you see how Anakin Skywalker is such a complex character. Was he ever truly evil? Yes, he was, but there's some redemption there, which appeals to the Catholic in me.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #56 (2001).

> Other elements to Watto's backstory included that he was a gambler, a rough customer who aspired to more wealth and social standing. To help convey that aspect of his personality, the animators squinted one eye to make him look more like a pirate. He was older and tended to be grumpy, a quality he demonstrates the first time Anakin arrives on screen. To show his age, animators decided that Watto would fly all the time because his legs hurt. If you look carefully, you can see him scratching his legs to relieve the pain in a couple of scenes. —Rob Coleman, Star Wars Insider 58, p. 73
> [fragment — full passage not on file]

> In the beginning of Episode II, Anakin Skywalker is clearly a good kid. He is a Padawan learner on his way to becoming a Jedi. Exceptionally talented, he is growing impatient with the fact that he's still an apprentice—making for an interesting relationship with his Master, Obi-Wan Kenobi. In many ways, Anakin is a typical 20-year-old—he wants to be in control of his life, yet he is not quite ready to be on his own. He is charming and charismatic, but he is also headstrong, rebellious, and impetuous. In Episode I, young Anakin had a crush on Queen Amidala. Ten years later, this crush has turned into love, even though romantic love is forbidden for a Jedi. Anakin is torn between his loyalty to the Jedi order and his uncontrollable passion for Padmé. —George Lucas, Star Wars Insider 59, p. 5
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Star Wars Insider #59 (2002).

> Attack of the Clones is more about the characters, and there's a clearer relationship between Anakin and Padmé. It's tense at the end, and you can feel the tension building as it goes toward the climax. The structure and pace are much better, and it's funnier. Also, it's much more intricate now. You can see where things are going to go in terms of Anakin. You can start to put the pieces together of how he got to be Darth Vader, and that's fun.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #60 (2002).

We were very specific when we wanted to show the dark side of Anakin.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #60 (2002).

> I spent the most time on learning the fight scene,
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> When I arrived in Sydney, I spent about a month learning how to play with a lightsaber. They were heavy—it was like wielding a real sword. I knew where every foot was supposed to go, and I knew it like the back of my hand, up to the point where it became second nature. He just embedded the routine into my head, so I couldn't mess up.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #60 (2002).

> The point is not that you are born evil—the thing that makes the film work ultimately is the fact that he is a good kid, trying to be a good kid, and he grows up to be a good kid. It's simply that his emotions take him places he can't control. He becomes evil out of his own ambition and greed, and revenge and hatred—all those things that kids face. Kids can see how their fears can cause them to do things that will turn them evil, even if they don't want to be evil. So hopefully, that will have some kind of an impact when it's all finished.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #60 (2002).

(hand-read)
Our notes: `notes/materials/038-star-wars-insider/061-ins061.md:180`

> The Imperial March in this case is a powerful portent, suggesting a very dramatic future which is in store for all of the characters in the next episode,
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> It's a piece of music that represents, in my mind at least, not only Darth Vader but the forces of the Empire. It was actually George's suggestion to use it there, and I think it works extremely well.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #61 (2002).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: John Williams on the Imperial March in Attack of the Clones: ""The Imperial March in this case is a powerful portent, suggesting a very dramatic future which is in store for all of the characters in the next episode," says Williams. "It's a piece of music that represents, in my mind at least, not only Darth Vader but the forces of the Empire. It was actually George's suggestion to use it there, and I think it works extremely well." —John Williams, Star Wars Insider 61, p. 27" Source: *Star Wars Insider #61* (2002), scan p.27

> That's because it's all one story,
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> It's the journey of a family. In this one we find out who Anakin really is and why he does the things that he does. In Episode I we meet little Anakin, and he's this perfect little boy, but in the music there's some minor chords which tell us, 'Uh-oh, something's not quite right. There's some damage here.' That's built on in Episode II, and that develops all the way through Episode III. So that by the time you get to Episode IV, you understand exactly where Anakin Skywalker has come from.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #61 (2002).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Rick McCallum on Anakin's music: ""That's because it's all one story," he says. "It's the journey of a family. In this one we find out who Anakin really is and why he does the things that he does. In Episode I we meet little Anakin, and he's this perfect little boy, but in the music there's some minor chords which tell us, 'Uh-oh, something's not quite right. There's some damage here.' That's built on in Episode II, and that develops all the way through Episode III. So that by the time you get to Episode IV, you understand exactly where Anakin Skywalker has come from." —Rick McCallum, Star Wars Insider 61, pp. 27, 29" Source: *Star Wars Insider #61* (2002), scan p.27
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #61* (2002), scan p.27 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins061-s27-thats-because-its-all-one-story.jpg)

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): Page contents: Across the Stars cont.: end of McCallum's Anakin quote; "Theme Music" on the new love theme (Lucas: never had a love theme before; Williams on star-crossed lovers separated by rank). (hand-read)
  — Source: Star Wars Insider #61 (2002).
Our notes: `notes/materials/038-star-wars-insider/061-ins061.md:194`

> It is a kind of love theme that has a sense of star-crossed destinies if you like, where the lovers in this case, not unlike Romeo and Juliet, are separated. In this case they are separated by rank: One is a senator, and the other is a soldier. Only perhaps at the end of their lives can they be joined together without any of the obstacles that the world sets forth. It gives a wonderfully compelling and dramatic and satisfying texture, even though in the end it's sad . . . . It can also be liberating . . . the idea of eternal love being something which extends beyond time.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #61 (2002).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker); Padmé Amidala — entry/section: John Williams on the Anakin-Padmé love theme: ""It is a kind of love theme that has a sense of star-crossed destinies if you like, where the lovers in this case, not unlike Romeo and Juliet, are separated. In this case they are separated by rank: One is a senator, and the other is a soldier. Only perhaps at the end of their lives can they be joined together without any of the obstacles that the world sets forth. It gives a wonderfully compelling and dramatic and satisfying texture, even though in the end it's sad . . . . It can also be liberating . . . the idea of eternal love being something which extends beyond time." —John Williams, Star Wars Insider 61, p. 29" Source: *Star Wars Insider #61* (2002), scan p.29
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #61* (2002), scan p.29 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins061-s29-it-is-a-kind-of-love.jpg)

> Anakin is overly confident and too sure of himself. A part of his mentality is, if he is the greatest Jedi ever, why does he have to follow anyone's guidance? It's that confidence that makes for his propulsion toward the dark side. I thought it was very well justified how he's going to go bad. I thought in many ways it paralleled how any human being would gradually, through their reaction to certain happenings, actually go bad.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #62 (2002); Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Hayden Christensen on Anakin.
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Hayden Christensen on Anakin: ""Anakin is overly confident and too sure of himself. ## Star Wars Insider #73 (2004)

> What happened with the droid factory is we realized—after reviewing the film during the pick-up shooting in London—that there was just too much plot. The [characters] arrived at the planet and were instantly captured. It wasn't exciting. So instead of saying, 'Well, we screwed up,' we decided to do something about it.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #73 (2004).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker); Padmé Amidala; C-3PO; R2-D2 — entry/section: McCallum on the droid factory: ""What happened with the droid factory is we realized—after reviewing the film during the pick-up shooting in London—that there was just too much plot. The [characters] arrived at the planet and were instantly captured. It wasn't exciting. So instead of saying, 'Well, we screwed up,' we decided to do something about it." —Rick McCallum, Star Wars Insider 73, p. 6" Source: *Star Wars Insider #73* (2004), scan p.6

> One of his first changes was to “Remove probe zaps snow squirrel,” while others were: “Perhaps Luke cuts wampa arm off … Search outtakes of Yoda throwing can … Double-print entire sequence [of dark side tree] … Play some of Yoda line, ‘You will destroy all that they have worked for’ over Luke … Add new wide shot Cloud City before Leia in VistaVision plate … Remove Vader line, ‘You have less power than you think’ … CS Vader with fist, ‘If you only knew the power of the dark side’ … Vader hollers, ‘Luke!’ when Luke falls … Extend head of Chewie growl to play over off-screen Luke line, ‘May the Force be with you.’ ”
>
> “What’s the screen direction of the object flying past you; was it coming toward you or away from you?” Lucas says. “Empire is a completely cinematic, editorial way of making a movie, which is where I come from.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #75 (2004).

> Every day you constantly have to keep bringing new and unique ideas. That's the tough part. In one year we created more than 5,000 pieces of concept art for the movie. In all my years of working in this industry, I have never seen this amount of art work for one feature film.
>
> Helping Anakin and Obi-Wan age and transform has been a designer's delight,
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> but Padmé's final incarnation was one of the most emotional creative challenges I've ever had to face.
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Star Wars Insider #76 (2004).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker); Obi-Wan Kenobi; Padmé Amidala — entry/section: Bridging to Episode IV: "Lee agrees. "Every day you constantly have to keep bringing new and unique ideas. That's the tough part. In one year we created more than 5,000 pieces of concept art for the movie. In all my years of working in this industry, I have never seen this amount of art work for one feature film." Another challenge for the Art Department was providing a visual "bridge" between Episodes III and IV. In McCaig's case, those "bridges" were major characters in the story. "Helping Anakin and Obi-Wan age and transform has been a designer's delight," he says, "but Padmé's final incarnation was one of the most emotional creative challenges I've ever had to face." —Sang Jun Lee and Iain McCaig, Star Wars Insider 76, p. 36" Source: *Star Wars Insider #76* (2004), scan p.36
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #76* (2004), scan p.36 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins076-s36-lee-agrees-every-day-you-constantly.jpg)

What it shows (our notes): Page contents: No. 6 opener: Luke and Vader in the Dagobah cave (pull quote "What's in there?" "Only what you take with you"). (hand-read)
> "There's something not right here," Luke tells Yoda. "1 feel cold, death." Yoda agrees. "That place ... is strong with the dark side of the Force," he explains to his student. "A domain of evil it is. In you must go." When Luke asks what's inside the cave, Yoda answers, "Only what you take with you." The dangers of the dark side cave on Dagobah aren't physical in nature. Instead, the cave plays on the fears and desires of those who come within the range of its influence, seeking to tempt or frighten them with strange visions and dire predictions, The interior of the underground cave is wet and slimy. It is dark and quiet. Here, amid the dangling roots and deep shadows, the dark side generates illusions to play upon the doubts and fears of those who enter the cave.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #77 (2004).
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from research primary dark side sourcebook dss (text copy `research_primary_dark_side_sourcebook_dss_pdf_9cd3453f4a`).)

> The Sith are the archenemies of the Jedi, and for a long time, they ruled the universe until the Jedi came along and got rid of them. The evil master Sith in all of the films is Darth Sidious, who becomes Emperor of the universe.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #78 (2004).

> In Revenge of the Sith, we have a more brotherly relationship—but certainly older brother to younger brother—and I refer to Anakin as being like my brother. Their relationship is complete now. You will notice this from the way we deal with situations—in battle, we're still joking with one another and looking out for one another. Very often, Anakin is saving my bacon when I'm knocked unconscious or whatever, or he steps in to help out when I've screwed up.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #81 (2005).

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): Page contents: Comlink continued: Star Wars TV series to be set between Episodes III and IV; clones become stormtroopers by A New Hope (see Ask the Master); original trilogy CD-ROM (Behind the Magic) and LucasArts' Insider's Guide to The Phantom Menace; Master Replicas Darth Vader sweepstakes (ends August 31, 2005). (hand-read)
  — Source: Star Wars Insider #82 (2005).
Our notes: `notes/materials/038-star-wars-insider/082-ins082.md:50`

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): Page contents: Lucas Q&A continued: TV series set between Episodes III and IV; Anakin Starkiller origins and the serial format; Lucas would be R2-D2, has red and blue lightsabers; thanks to fans; "Rick McCallum's Spectacular Spectacular" Episode III footage and ILM reel in the 500 Ballroom (40-foot screen). (hand-read)
  — Source: Star Wars Insider #82 (2005).
Our notes: `notes/materials/038-star-wars-insider/082-ins082.md:78`

> I started with Anakin Starkiller and his two kids, then I got to a draft that's kind of what we know now, but it was 200 pages. Then that became three films. I never thought I'd go back and do the original stories, regardless of what the press says or what's out there. But then the necessary film technology came along and I came to grips with being known forever as George 'Star Wars' Lucas.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #82 (2005).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker); Luke Skywalker — entry/section: Anakin Starkiller and the serial: ""There was originally to be one film. I wanted it to be Episode IV of a serial (maybe out of 12 or something) that you would never see the first or last episodes of. That's what happened in the 1930s; you had trailers, a short cartoon, and then a cliffhanger. That serial style is what Star Wars is based on. "I started with Anakin Starkiller and his two kids, then I got to a draft that's kind of what we know now, but it was 200 pages. Then that became three films. I never thought I'd go back and do the original stories, regardless of what the press says or what's out there. But then the necessary film technology came along and I came to grips with being known forever as George 'Star Wars' Lucas." —George Lucas, Star Wars Insider 82, p. 13" Source: *Star Wars Insider #82* (2005), scan p.13
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #82* (2005), scan p.13 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins082-s13-there-was-originally-to-be-one.jpg)
Our notes: `notes/materials/038-star-wars-insider/082-ins082.md:82`

> I've taken you now
> [fragment — full passage not on file]

> I've taken you now.
> [fragment — full passage not on file]

— Source: Star Wars Insider #82 (2005); Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section.
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: "I've taken you now": ""Yes. I had a scene where Nick plays a Jedi instructor, and I go in and kill him and these two [Padawans]. It was another one of those cases of the Master and the apprentice, and the apprentice overthrowing the Master. It was my scene to say, "I've taken you now." We just had so much fun with it. I clipped him right in the forehead and gave him a little gash with my lightsaber, which was a total accident. He thinks it was intentional, but I didn't mean to do it. In the next take, he smacked me in the head and got me back." —Hayden Christensen, Star Wars Insider 82, p. 49" Source: *Star Wars Insider #82* (2005), scan p.49
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #82* (2005), scan p.49 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins082-s49-yes-i-had-a-scene-where.jpg)

> I think it's impossible not to damage his mystique. The reason Vader resonated so much with audiences was because he represented the unknown. He did all these evil things and talked in a very villainous way, but outside of that you knew nothing about him. And it's [because of] the unknown that the mind is able to infer all of your worst fears. I think it is inevitable that some of the mystique no longer will be mystical—it's now informed.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #82 (2005); Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Vader's mystique now informed.

> Your eyes are contact lenses
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> In a sense, your eyes are contact lenses.
>
> —Ian McDiarmid, Star Wars Insider 82, p. 60
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Star Wars Insider #82 (2005).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Palpatine (Darth Sidious); Mace Windu; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: "Your eyes are contact lenses": ""He said, "In a sense, your eyes are contact lenses." In other words, the Palpatine character was the most artificial—it was as if they had grafted his face and put in (his) eyes. Because the real Palpatine is the one who bursts forth at a calculated moment in Episode III just after persuading Anakin to kill Mace (Windu). That is when the true person comes out, letting the evil fully manifest itself. The Emperor that you see in the last film looks like he does because he's very old and very evil—it is what he always looked like." —Ian McDiarmid, Star Wars Insider 82, p. 60" Source: *Star Wars Insider #82* (2005), scan p.60
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #82* (2005), scan p.60 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins082-s60-he-said-in-a-sense-your.jpg)

> George wanted a visually interesting antagonist who represented the technology that will ultimately consume Anakin,
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> It's an early version of the technology used to save Vader but in Grievous's case, the technology is not quite working. So, as formidable as he is, he's also a bit sickly. He's a skulking, miserable villain with a hacking cough, and we had a great time working with him because we love creating bad guys!
— Source: Star Wars Insider #85 (2006).
Note (our notes, around the quote): General Grievous; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Rob Coleman on Grievous: ""George wanted a visually interesting antagonist who represented the technology that will ultimately consume Anakin," says Coleman. "It's an early version of the technology used to save Vader but in Grievous's case, the technology is not quite working. So, as formidable as he is, he's also a bit sickly. He's a skulking, miserable villain with a hacking cough, and we had a great time working with him because we love creating bad guys!" —Rob Coleman, Star Wars Insider 85, p. 30" Source: *Star Wars Insider #85* (2006), scan p.30
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #85* (2006), scan p.30 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins085-s30-george-wanted-a-visually-interesting-antagonist.jpg)
Our notes: `notes/materials/038-star-wars-insider/085-ins085.md:183`

> Padmé is so naive and idealistic that she nearly wastes her planet because she thinks if she follows the rules, it will work out. And here she is now at the end of her life making the same mistakes. She's still idealistic. She gives Anakin too much credit. She believes her love can redeem him—even at the end, she believes it. Padmé's fault is she doesn't realize people break the rules.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #85 (2006); Padmé Amidala — entry/section: Iain McCaig on Padmé.
Note (our notes, around the quote): Padmé Amidala — entry/section: Iain McCaig on Padmé: ""Padmé is so naive and idealistic that she nearly wastes her planet because she thinks if she follows the rules, it will work out. > You can't do a whole series without meeting new people - that would be boring! In every Star Wars movie, you're usually meeting a host of new characters. It's always a toss up, as you want to use the characters the fans know and love, like Anakin Skywalker, [but] you also want to use ones they've never heard of before as that makes it interesting.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #95 (2007); Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: New characters.

> I probably watch Return of the Jedi the most. My favorites, in order, are Return of the Jedi, Empire second, Phantom Menace third, Star Wars, Sith and then Clones. I love Jedi the most because that's when I had all the toys. Those are the Christmases I remember the most. I think it's quite an underrated film. Everything happens in that one. That whole 20 minutes with Luke and the Emperor is scary! And Vader takes his mask off and he looks horrible! And you realize that he's just a man. I was surprised he was English.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #95 (2007).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker); Palpatine (Darth Sidious) — entry/section: Underrated Jedi: ""I probably watch Return of the Jedi the most. My favorites, in order, are Return of the Jedi, Empire second, Phantom Menace third, Star Wars, Sith and then Clones. I love Jedi the most because that's when I had all the toys. Those are the Christmases I remember the most. I think it's quite an underrated film. Everything happens in that one. That whole 20 minutes with Luke and the Emperor is scary! And Vader takes his mask off and he looks horrible! And you realize that he's just a man. I was surprised he was English." —Andrew White, Star Wars Insider 95, p. 16" Source: *Star Wars Insider #95* (2007), scan p.16

> Vader’s seat rotated, allowing him to face a wide viewscreen. It flicked on and displayed an image of Admiral Ozzel and Captain Piett on the Executor’s bridge. Ozzel turned his face and said, “Lord Vader, the fleet has moved out of lightspeed, and we’re preparing to — aaagh!”
— Source: Star Wars Insider #95 (2007); Admiral Kendal Ozzel — entry/section: Ozzel teaser.
(Fragment completed 2026-11-17 with the full excerpt, word for word from Star Wars®: Episode V: The Empire Strikes Back™ (by Ryder Windham, created by George Lucas) (text copy `MEGA_The_Episode_Junior_Novelizations_Star_Wars_Episode_V_The_Empire_Strikes_Back_Junior_Novelization_Ryder_Windham_epub`).)

> That was George Lucas' idea from the beginning, that Grievous would be coughing. We kind of questioned it, because we weren't sure what that meant for the story. Did it have something to do with Grievous' backstory? How did it connect to the history of the character? We were a little puzzled, but we do what George wants. He wanted Grievous to have these breathing troubles because he is a forerunner of the type of technology that eventually creates Darth Vader.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #99 (2008); Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Grievous' cough.
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Grievous' cough: ""That was George Lucas' idea from the beginning, that Grievous would be coughing. We kind of questioned it, because we weren't sure what that meant for the story. Did it have something to do with Grievous' backstory? How did it connect to the history of the character? We were a little puzzled, but we do what George wants. He wanted Grievous to have these breathing troubles because he is a forerunner of the type of technology that eventually creates Darth Vader." —Ben Burtt, Star Wars Insider 99, p. 47" Source: *Star Wars Insider #99* (2008), scan p.47
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #99* (2008), scan p.47 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins099-s47-that-was-george-lucas-idea-from.jpg)

> Setting up a long running series in the era is tricky. There's only so much you can do with the big three before you completely strain all credibility. But it's not impossible. We sowed some seeds toward the end of our Empire series run that blossomed into 'My Brother, My Enemy' and left open a lot of possibilities for future stories. Our upcoming Vector crossover will leave some significant ripples, particularly concerning Luke and Leia's interactions with Darth Vader.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #99 (2008).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Luke Skywalker; Leia Organa Solo; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Vector ripples: ""Setting up a long running series in the era is tricky. We sowed some seeds toward the end of our Empire series run that blossomed into 'My Brother, My Enemy' and left open a lot of possibilities for future stories. Our upcoming Vector crossover will leave some significant ripples, particularly concerning Luke and Leia's interactions with Darth Vader." —Jeremy Barlow, Star Wars Insider 99, p. 57" Source: *Star Wars Insider #99* (2008), scan p.57

> But over time his goals and motivations change. The Apprentice is joined on his journey by a fairly large cast, including Juno Eclipse, the Imperial pilot handpicked by Darth Vader to fly the character on his missions, and PROXY, the character's droid sidekick. Princess Leia and a few other known characters make key appearances as well, and of course Vader has a major role to play as the Apprentice's mentor and warped father figure.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #100 (2008).

> George provided us with a wealth of feedback on the type of master Vader would be, giving us insight into Vader's secret desires and plans, his relationship with the Emperor, and the methods he'd use to motivate an apprentice. George also weighed in heavily on how we planned to use some of the other known characters, such as Leia, and encouraged us to invent new characters for key roles—a love interest, a sidekick, a mentor beyond Vader. Aside from a few minor points—we couldn't call the Apprentice 'Darth' for example—neither George nor Lucas Licensing put many restrictions on the scripts.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #100 (2008).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker); Palpatine (Darth Sidious); Galen Marek — entry/section: Blackman on George Lucas's input: ""George provided us with a wealth of feedback on the type of master Vader would be, giving us insight into Vader's secret desires and plans, his relationship with the Emperor, and the methods he'd use to motivate an apprentice. George also weighed in heavily on how we planned to use some of the other known characters, such as Leia, and encouraged us to invent new characters for key roles—a love interest, a sidekick, a mentor beyond Vader. Aside from a few minor points—we couldn't call the Apprentice 'Darth' for example—neither George nor Lucas Licensing put many restrictions on the scripts." —Haden Blackman, Star Wars Insider 100, p. 70" Source: *Star Wars Insider #100* (2008), scan p.70

> We wanted to focus on this concept of the Force unleashed. My directive to the team was to start with the Force push. I wanted a Force push that doesn't just knock enemies over, it sends them flying backwards, shattering stuff. Once we got that right with the concept art, that became the foundation for all our Force powers. Because the Apprentice was trained by Vader—who's got a very unique perspective because he was Anakin Skywalker and trained as a Jedi—he also has access to dark side powers. There are also a bunch of new powers as well that are extensive. Force repulse is a power that sends out a kinetic shock wave that destroys everything around you, sending guys flying.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #100 (2008).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Galen Marek; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Blackman on Force powers: ""We wanted to focus on this concept of the Force unleashed. My directive to the team was to start with the Force push. I wanted a Force push that doesn't just knock enemies over, it sends them flying backwards, shattering stuff. Once we got that right with the concept art, that became the foundation for all our Force powers. Because the Apprentice was trained by Vader—who's got a very unique perspective because he was Anakin Skywalker and trained as a Jedi—he also has access to dark side powers. There are also a bunch of new powers as well that are extensive. Force repulse is a power that sends out a kinetic shock wave that destroys everything around you, sending guys flying." —Haden Blackman, Star Wars Insider 100, p. 72" Source: *Star Wars Insider #100* (2008), scan p.72

> Luke could turn Vader only because of the ties between them. We've seen just how merciless and dangerous Krayt can get when mad. Cade understands that, so long as Krayt lives, the Sith Order will be intact and Cade will never know peace. Cade is not concerned about redeeming Krayt or about saving the galaxy, when you come right down to it. He wants people to leave him alone. He wants to live his life on his own terms, not anyone else's. Krayt needs to die. Now, whether or not he can do that is an entirely different question. Cade's gotten himself in over his head before. We'll see if it happens again.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #101 (2008).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Luke Skywalker; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker); Cade Skywalker; Darth Krayt — entry/section: Ostrander: Krayt needs to die: "Adds Ostrander, "Luke could turn Vader only because of the ties between them. > GtO ss** cinematographer the person in charge of running the camera and filming the movie crew all of the people involved in the making of a movie diabetes a disorder in which the body cannot use sugar properly director the person in charge of every aspect of a film documentary a nonfiction film meant to be educational editor a person who cuts and rearranges film or text into its final form grip the person in charge of props and carrying film equipment icon a character that people admire postproduction the addition of sound, music, and special effects after a movie has been filmed science fiction a story involving setting, characters, or technology that does not exist score the music created for a movie serial a story divided into several parts and told over a period of time special effects ways of making unreal or impossible situations believable trilogy a series of three films or three books $ou *of* o* „cts While researching information for this book, Chris and Dana Rau found it was the perfect time to be doing a biography on George Lucas. With the release of the Star Wars Special Editions both in theaters and video cassette in 1997, and the all-new Star Wars movie in 1999, Lucas was often in the news.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #103 (2008).

> Sound Bites! Star Wars folk speak up!
— Source: Star Wars Insider #104 (2008); Page contents: Launch Pad (folio 14).

> The Clone Wars has all of the characters everybody knows—from Yoda to Anakin to Mace Windu to Obi-Wan—they're all there. The live-action series, meanwhile, has nobody [you'll know], because it's after Episode III, so everybody's dead, basically, or hiding somewhere. You hear about the Emperor, just like you do in Episode IV, but it's mostly about a whole different world. I mean, there are a million stories in the big city—you've only seen one of them.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #104 (2008); none — entry/section: Lucas on the live-action series.

> The animation looks great. I am not sure how long the series will go, but we will see shades of Anakin's dark side as we go along.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #104 (2008).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Matt Lanter on his Anakin: ""This will be a very different Anakin than fans are used to as this is before he really started the dark spiral that was in the films. He is much more of a leader and hero in the film as well as a mentor and friend. He is a very likeable individual and I think the audience will see him in a new light, which is what George Lucas wanted, a tragic fall of a true hero. "The animation looks great. I am not sure how long the series will go, but we will see shades of Anakin's dark side as we go along." —Matt Lanter, Star Wars Insider 104, p. 8" Source: *Star Wars Insider #104* (2008), scan p.8

Note (our notes, around the quote): Galen Marek; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Blackman on the apprentice as a "superhero": ""Some of the germs for The Force Unleashed were certainly in the Wookiee concept: this idea that you were a superhero in the Star Wars universe," Blackman says. > As it stands now we've got three scenes with the light sword. The first scene shows what it is: Luke just turns it on. The next one is a very quick scene in the cantina—it'll just be a flash. In both those cases, it's just one sword. The last is the final battle between Ben and the warlord. That's going to be the tricky one where they actually fight, but at least now it is down to a very controlled set-up.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #104 (2008).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Obi-Wan Kenobi; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Lucas on the light sword scenes: ""As it stands now we've got three scenes with the light sword. The first scene shows what it is: Luke just turns it on. The next one is a very quick scene in the cantina—it'll just be a flash. In both those cases, it's just one sword. The last is the final battle between Ben and the warlord. That's going to be the tricky one where they actually fight, but at least now it is down to a very controlled set-up." —George Lucas (The Making of Star Wars), Star Wars Insider 104, p. 70" Source: *Star Wars Insider #104* (2008), scan p.70
![Quote image: *Star Wars Insider #104* (2008), scan p.70 — full quote as printed](quotes/ins104-s70-as-it-stands-now-weve-got.jpg)

> Proxy's a very dangerous droid, who is programmed to kill the apprentice, but is all smiles while he's trying to do it. He doesn't understand death and it's this lethal versus loyal dichotomy that makes him unique and interesting. Star Wars fans have never seen what Sith training is really like. To see PROXY you get a glimpse of what it would be like to be raised in terror, because your best friend may try to kill you in your sleep. When he fails he says he's sorry, and when Vader's not there they're friends like kids in a playground.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #106 (2009).
Note (our notes, around the quote): PROXY (droid); Galen Marek; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: David W. Collins on PROXY: ""Proxy's a very dangerous droid, who is programmed to kill the apprentice, but is all smiles while he's trying to do it. He doesn't understand death and it's this lethal versus loyal dichotomy that makes him unique and interesting. Star Wars fans have never seen what Sith training is really like. To see PROXY you get a glimpse of what it would be like to be raised in terror, because your best friend may try to kill you in your sleep. When he fails he says he's sorry, and when Vader's not there they're friends like kids in a playground." —David W. Collins, Star Wars Insider 106, p. 19" Source: *Star Wars Insider #106* (2009), scan p.19

> Dave's vision for Obi-Wan in this series is very specific. He's trying to show Anakin the right way, but he's also the calm in the storm. I'm always the straight guy. Most of the time, I'm pulling the reins back on Anakin and Ahsoka, so I'm like the older brother. Nothing really gets to him.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #112 (2009).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Obi-Wan Kenobi; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker); Ahsoka Tano — entry/section: Taylor on Filoni's vision for Obi-Wan: ""Dave's vision for Obi-Wan in this series is very specific. He's trying to show Anakin the right way, but he's also the calm in the storm. I'm always the straight guy. Most of the time, I'm pulling the reins back on Anakin and Ahsoka, so I'm like the older brother. Nothing really gets to him." —James Arnold Taylor, Star Wars Insider 112, p. 26" Source: *Star Wars Insider #112* (2009), scan p.26

> Vader and the Emperor have wiped out most of them, so it would be hard for Vader to go find an ex-Jedi or a Jedi-in-hiding and convert them to being his apprentice. Vader put all his eggs in one basket originally with Starkiller; he stole him away from Kashyyyk and trained him for 14 years or so.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #118 (2010).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Galen Marek; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Blackman on why Vader needs Starkiller: ""Vader and the Emperor have wiped out most of them, so it would be hard for Vader to go find an ex-Jedi or a Jedi-in-hiding and convert them to being his apprentice. > But unlike Vader who is doing evil and knows it, or Palpatine who is doing evil and loves it, with Boba (or Jango) it's all business. He may know that by taking you in for the bounty on your head that he's ruining the lives of your entire family, but it's never personal. He neither regrets nor relishes the fallout from his actions. He's really more of an anti-hero than a true bad guy.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #119 (2010).

## Star Wars Insider #120 (2010)

> All those months of attacking Imperial establishments and Vader sends a boy?

> —Cully Fredricksen, Star Wars Insider 120, p. 26
> [fragment — full passage not on file]

Note (our notes, around the quote): Rahm Kota; Galen Marek; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Fredricksen on Starkiller defeating and blinding Kota: ""Kota is trying to lure Darth Vader out of hiding, and he encounters Starkiller. Kota says something to the effect of, "All those months of attacking Imperial establishments and Vader sends a boy?" It's, like, "C'mon... really?" Well, then that boy goes on to totally kick my ass—blinding me in the process—and that drives me to a spaceport near Bespin to drink away my sorrows." —Cully Fredricksen, Star Wars Insider 120, p. 26" Source: *Star Wars Insider #120* (2010), scan p.26
